## Document 121: 261016_gs_WNC

Source PDF: all report pdf/261016_gs_WNC.pdf

### Page 2

Earnings revision: We factor in WNC 2Q26 result and revise up earnings by 1%/ 2%/ 2% 
in 2026-28E to reflect higher than expected revenues of WiFi 7/ WiFi 8 and switch 
products, driven by spec upgrade and rising client demand. We keep GM and Opex ratio 
largely unchanged.

Valuation: With the earning estimate changes, we raise our 12M TP to NT$380.0 (vs. 
previously at NT$371, based on 28.0x 2027E P/E, unchanged). We continue to base 
our TP on 2027E P/E, and the target multiple is still derived from the correlation 
between P/E and EPS growth of its peers, which is higher as we mark to market. Our 
target P/E of 28.0x is higher than company’s avg. +1 std P/E (16.3x), which we believe is 
not stretched considering the sustainable growth of WNC driven by long-term satellite 
and high speed networking demand. Maintain Buy.

### Page 3

Price Target Risks and Methodology: WNC 
Valuation: We use a near-term P/E to derive our 12m target price for WNC, consistent 
with our Taiwan Technology coverage. We base our TP of NT$380.0 on a target P/E 
multiple of 28.0x on a forward year EPS (2027E). Our target P/E is derived from the 
correlation between P/E and EPS growth of its peers. We are Buy rated on WNC. Key 
downside risks: fiercer-than-expected competition in satellite communication, 
slower-than-expected Wi-Fi 7 or 5G FWA ramp-up in US and Europe markets.

Source: Company data, Goldman Sachs Research estimates, FactSet. Price as of 15 Oct 2026 close.

Ratings, coverage universe and related definitions
Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.
Coverage Universe: A list of all stocks in each coverage universe is available by primary analyst, stock and coverage universe at

https://www.gs.com/research/hedge.html.

Goldman Sachs is acting in an advisory capacity in a merger or in a strategic transaction involving this company, when there are legal, regulatory or
policy constraints due to Goldman Sachs’ involvement in a transaction, and in certain other circumstances. Early-Stage Biotech (ES). An investment

rating and a target price are not assigned pursuant to Goldman Sachs policy when this company has neither a drug, treatment or medical device that

upon.  Coverage Suspended (CS). Goldman Sachs has suspended coverage of this company.  Not Covered (NC). Goldman Sachs does not cover this
company.

## Document 199: [207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 파업 영향에도 견조한 실적, 4분기 성장 확대삼성바이오로직스(207940) 파업 영향에도 견조한 실적, 4분기_22fa3b02

Source PDF: all report pdf/[207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 파업 영향에도 견조한 실적, 4분기 성장 확대삼성바이오로직스(207940) 파업 영향에도 견조한 실적, 4분기_22fa3b02.pdf

투자의견 BUY  
매수, 유지 
6개월 목표주가 2,000,000 
유지  
현재주가 
(26.10.21) 
1,417,000 
제약업종

◼ 3Q26 파업 영향에도 컨센서스 상회 
◼ 4Q26 지연 물량 인식 및 신규 공장 기여로 실적 개선 전망 
◼ 투자의견 매수, 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지

투자의견 매수, 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지 
목표주가는 DCF 방식으로 산출(WACC 8.1%, 영구성장률 3.5% 적용). 기
존 공장의 높은 가동률을 기반으로 5공장 매출 기여 확대, 미국 생산거점 
확보, 신규 모달리티 진출을 통한 중장기 성장 지속 전망.

3Q26 Review: 파업 영향에도 컨센서스 상회  
3Q26 매출액 1조 4,903억원(+18.5% yoy, +12.8% qoq), 영업이익 6,401
억원(+1.1% yoy, +9.1% qoq), 영업이익률 42.9%(-7.5%p yoy)를 기록하며 
당사 추정치 및 시장 컨센서스 상회. 5월 1차 파업에 따른 매출 차질(약 
1,500억원) 에도 불구하고, 기존 1~4공장 Full 가동이 지속되며 견조한 실
적 달성. 5공장 PPQ 배치 생산 확대와 및 미국 록빌 공장 매출 인식도 외
형 성장에 기여. 다만, 신규 생산시설 초기 가동비와 제품 믹스 영향으로 
영업이익률은 전년 동기 대비 하락.

4Q26 매출액 1조 5,764억원(+22.6% yoy), 영업이익 7,075억원
(+34.0% yoy) 전망  
4Q26에는 2분기 파업으로 지연된 생산물량의 순차적인 매출 인식과 미국 
록빌 공장 매출 기여, 5공장 PPQ 배치 생산 확대로 실적 개선 예상, 동사
는 신규 생산시설의 매출 가시성 확보를 바탕으로 2026년 매출 성장률 
20%를 무난히 달성할 것으로 전망(연초 가이던스 15~20%).

신규 수주 회복과 포트폴리오 확장에 주목 
미국 의약품 관세정책 불확실성 완화로 지연됐던 수주 협상이 재개되며 신
규 수주 환경 개선 중. 3Q26 약 3억달러의 신규 수주를 확보했으며, 대형 
계약 협상도 구체화되고 있어 2027년 수주 회복 기대. 이는 5공장 가동률 
확대와 중장기 성장 가시성을 높이는 요인. 6공장은 ‘26년말~’27년초 착
공을 목표로 기존 증설 일정 유지. 
폴리펩타이드 그룹 인수는 1차 공개매수 응모율 87.88%를 기록하며 연내 
인수 완료 추진 중. 인수 완료 시 펩타이드 CDMO로 사업영역이 확대되며 
글로벌 생산거점 및 고객 기반 다변화 기대.

산업 투자의견 
- Overweight(비중확대) 
  : 향후 6개월간 업종지수상승률이 시장수익률 대비 초과 상승 예상 
- Neutral(중립) 
  : 향후 6개월간 업종지수상승률이 시장수익률과 유사한 수준 예상 
- Underweight(비중축소) 
  : 향후 6개월간 업종지수상승률이 시장수익률 대비 하회 예상

기업 투자의견 
- Buy(매수)  
  : 향후 6개월간 시장수익률 대비 10%p 이상 주가 상승 예상 
- Marketperform(시장수익률) 
  : 향후 6개월간 시장수익률 대비 -10%p~10%p 주가 변동 예상 
- Underperform(시장수익률 하회) 
  : 향후 6개월간 시장수익률 대비 10%p 이상 주가 하락 예상

### Page 1

2026.09.21   
LG에너지솔루션 
(373220)

원-달러 환율 하락에도 실적 개선세 지속 Company Brief

3Q26 원/달러 환율 하락에도 불구하고 영업이익 시장 기대치 상회 전망 
동사의 3Q26 실적은 매출 8.8조원(+45% YoY, + 16% QoQ), 영업이익 3,690억원(-
39% YoY, +226% QoQ)을 기록하며 원/달러 환율 하락으로 매출은 시장 기대치를 
소폭 하회하겠으나 영업이익은 상회할 것으로 전망된다. AMPC 는 미국 ESS 생산량 
증가와 GM 얼티엄셀즈 재가동에 힘입어 전분기 대비 약 57% 증가한 3,790억원으로 
추정된다. 미국 상호관세 위법 판결에 따른 관세 환급금이나 완성차 업체들의 최소 
구매 물량 미달 보상금 반영 여부에 따라 추가적인 이익 개선 가능성도 존재한다. ESS 
부문은 전분기에서 이연된 물량의 출하가 본격화되면서 3Q26 매출이 전분기 대비 약 
38% 증가할 것으로 예상된다. 특히 미국 ESS 생산라인의 가동 안정화와 AMPC 수혜 
확대에 힘입어 수익성도 점진적으로 개선될 것으로 보인다. 자동차전지 부문도 
3Q26부터 회복세가 강화될 것으로 예상된다. 유럽향 물량 회복과 GM 얼티엄셀즈 
재가동으로 매출이 전분기 대비 큰 폭으로 증가하고, 고정비 부담 완화로 적자폭도 
뚜렷하게 축소될 전망이다. 소형전지 부문은 주요 고객사인 Tesla의 판매 호조에 따른 
안정적인 출하 증가로 매출 회복과 수익성 방어가 가능할 것으로 예상된다. 
4Q26 대규모 ESS 신규 수주 기대 
전세계 ESS 시장은 신재생 에너지 발전 확대와 AIDC 투자 본격화에 힘입어 빠르게 
성장하고 있다. 시장조사기관 SNE리서치에 따르면 1H26 전세계 ESS 배터리 
출하량은 461GWh로 전년 동기 대비 약 71% 증가했다. 이 중 북미 출하량은 
76GWh로 전년 동기 대비 약 83% 증가했으며, 분기별로는 1Q26 35GWh(+91% 
YoY), 2Q26 41GWh(+76% YoY) 를 기록했다. 통상 하반기에 출하가 집중되는 
계절성을 감안하면 올해 북미 ESS 배터리 출하량은 전년 대비 약 70% 증가한 
170~180GWh에 이를 것으로 전망된다. 연초 동사가 예상한 올해 북미 ESS 수요 
성장률 10% 초반 수준을 크게 상회할 가능성이 높은 상황이다. 올해 동사의 ESS 
신규 수주 목표는 약 90GWh이며, 현재까지 약 30GWh의 수주를 확정한 것으로 
파악된다. 이에 시장 일각에서는 연간 수주 목표 달성 여부에 대한 우려가 제기되고 
있다. 그러나 당사가 파악한 바에 따르면 대규모 ESS 신규 수주가 4Q26에 집중될 
것으로 예상된다. 특히 북미 ESS 수요의 가파른 증가세와 투자세액공제(48E)에 
연계된 PFE 공급망 규제 시행은 동사의 수주 확보에 우호적으로 작용할 전망이다. 
매수 투자의견과 목표주가 유지 
동사에 대한 매수 투자의견과 목표주가를 유지한다. 향후 국내 배터리 셀 업체들의 
북미 ESS 신규 수주가 지속적으로 확대될 전망이다. 미국 ESS 시장에서는 신재생 
에너지 발전 확대와 AIDC 투자 본격화에 따른 수요 증가, 중국 공급망에 대한 규제 
강화가 맞물리면서 국내 업체들에 우호적인 사업 환경이 조성되고 있다. 동사 역시 
ESS 중심의 생산라인 전환과 가동률 상승이 본격화될 경우 매출 성장과 함께 고정비 
부담 완화, AMPC 수혜 확대에 따른 수익성 개선이 기대된다. 이를 바탕으로 동사의 
영업이익은 2026년 약 0.8조원에서 2028년 약 5.1조원으로 가파르게 증가할 것으로 
전망된다. ESS의 역할이 신재생 에너지 연계용 저장장치를 넘어 AIDC의 안정적인 
전력 공급을 지원하는 핵심 인프라로 확대되고 있다는 점에도 주목할 필요가 있다. 
이에 따라 동사의 밸류에이션 역시 기존 EV 중심의 평가에서 벗어나 전력 인프라 
산업의 성장성을 반영하는 방향으로 재평가되어야 한다고 판단된다. 업종 내에서 실적 
개선 가시성과 정책 수혜가 뚜렷한 동사에 대한 비중 확대 전략이 유효하다. 
Buy (Maintain)

본 분석자료는 투자자의 증권투자를 돕기 위한 참고자료이며, 따라서, 본 자료에 의한 투자자의 투자결과에 대해 어떠한 목적의 증빙자료로도 사용될 수 없으며, 어떠한 경우에도 작성자 및 
당사의 허가 없이 전재, 복사 또는 대여될 수 없습니다. 무단전재 등으로 인한 분쟁발생시 법적 책임이 있음을 주지하시기 바랍니다.

## Document 228: Asia week ahead_ BoJ rates, China’s Fifth Plenum, Taiwan and Korea data

Source PDF: all report pdf/Asia week ahead_ BoJ rates, China’s Fifth Plenum, Taiwan and Korea data.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-23
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Bank of Japan is expected to hold rates at 1.25%, while
Taiwan’s economy is set to record another quarter of double-
digit growth. Markets will also focus on China’s Fifth Plenum
meeting and South Korea’s third-quarter GDP data

Deepali Bhargava and Lynn Song
Asia Research highlights of the week
China’s GDP edges higher as activity stabilised in September
Japan: BoJ expected to hold rates at 1.25%
We expect the Bank of Japan to keep its policy rate unchanged at
1.25% on Friday as it assesses the impact of last month’s 25bp
hike. Our base case assumes two additional 25bp hikes in January
and April 2027, taking the policy rate to 1.75%. While inﬂation
remains elevated, policymakers may prefer more time to monitor
the economy, particularly as the government continues to support
domestic demand.

### Page 2

Japan will release its October Tokyo inﬂation data and September
retail sales and industrial production on Friday. Market consensus is
for headline inﬂation to remain unchanged at 2.7% year-on-year,
while core inﬂation eases slightly to 2.6%. Retail sales growth is
expected to accelerate to 3.3% YoY, while industrial production
rebounds by 1.3% month-on-month following August’s sharp
decline.
China: Fifth Plenum takes centre stage
The key event for China is the Fifth Plenary Session of the 20th

Chinese Communist Party Central Committee, taking place in
Beijing between 26-29 October. The focus will likely be political
rather than economic, but markets will scrutinise the communiqué
for new signals. China will also release September industrial proﬁts
data. We’ll be watching closely to see whether August’s slowdown
deepened or began to reverse.
Taiwan: Double-digit GDP growth expected
to continue
Taiwan will release third-quarter GDP data on Friday. We’re looking

for another quarter of double-digit growth, forecasting 11.4% YoY,
amid strong external demand. Strong retail sales growth over the
past few months suggests domestic demand will hold up as well.
South Korea: Growth expected to remain
resilient
South Korea releases third-quarter GDP data on Tuesday. Market
consensus expects growth to moderate to 3.2% YoY from 3.7% in
the second quarter, while sequential growth edges up to 0.8%
quarter-on-quarter. Continued strength in semiconductor demand

should support underlying momentum. A solid reading would
reinforce the Bank of Korea’s view that economic growth remains
resilient.

## Document 242: Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-14)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-14).pdf
Report source: Merrill Lynch (CMO)
Date: 2026-09-14
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook
September 14, 2026
All data, projections and opinions are as of the date of this report and subject to change.
IN THIS ISSUE
Macro Strategy—Superintelligence and Superabundance in the Next Decade:
Rapid advances in Artificial Intelligence (AI) have prompted prominent architects of
the new economy to conclude the Singularity is arriving ahead of schedule as signs
of its effects proliferate in the multitude of new AI models running in the increasing

number of data centers driving one of the biggest investment booms in U.S. history.
Superintelligence that surpasses collective human intelligence has long been one of the
predictions of futurists analyzing the implications of the Singularity. Superabundance is
one of the results of Superintelligence predicted by those who have written about the
consequences of this major acceleration in technological innovation. The early arrival of
these radical forces for change poses imminent challenges for society and investors that

will shape the next decade in critical ways.
In particular, the heightened uncertainty about how this new era plays out increases the
utility of a well-diversified approach to wealth management in our view.
Market View—Elevated Debt Supply Is Testing the Market's Appetite: The recent
rise in Treasury yields reflects a confluence of factors, including Federal Reserve (Fed)
policy uncertainty, deteriorating fiscal dynamics, resilient economic growth, persistent

inflation concerns and a record wave of debt issuance from both the U.S. Treasury and
corporate borrowers, particularly in the long end of the curve. Importantly, we view this
as a period of market adjustment rather than a cause for alarm.
Episodes of debt pressures can be a natural consequence of elevated supply, in addition
to other external factors and could be resolved through higher yields that help balance
supply and demand. While interest rate volatility may remain elevated and long-term

yields could settle at structurally higher levels, today's combination of attractive nominal
and real yields presents a potential opportunity for investors to extend beyond cash and
short-duration holdings and move closer to strategic duration targets.
The Ticker Tape—Top Questions Answered Directly by Our Chief Investment
Officer (CIO).
• What are the markets actually telling us?
AUTHORS
Chief Investment Office
Macro Strategy Team
Chief Investment Office

Fixed Income Strategy Team
Christopher Hyzy

Portfolio Considerations
We view recent volatility as a rolling
correction rather than the end of
the cycle. We favor diversified Equity
exposure, with potential opportunities
extending beyond Growth to Value,
Small- and Mid-caps, Non-U.S. Equities
(especially Emerging Markets), dividend-
focused and thematic strategies.

We favor adjusting portfolios toward
strategic durations given attractive
elevated nominal and real yields, and see
value in high-quality bonds, particularly
longer-dated municipal bonds.
Consider broader portfolio
diversification with potential
opportunities across industrial metals,
energy, Real/Tangible Assets, Hedge
Funds, private markets and global
strategies to capture themes from AI,
reindustrialization, infrastructure growth
and market dispersion.

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Superintelligence and Superabundance in the Next Decade
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
While some academics have claimed the Singularity is still off in the distant future, or even
unlikely at all, those on the front lines of AI model development have recently asserted that it
has already begun. For example, Elon Musk has pointed to recent issues with AI models going

outside their proscribed limits, including hacking external systems and completing previously
unsolved math problems. Over a year ago, Sam Altman wrote, "We are past the event horizon; the
takeoff has started. Humanity is close to building superintelligence..." In short, the rapid evolution
of these models has convinced knowledgeable observers that the Singularity has begun.
Superintelligence is the primary driver of the Singularity as exponential advances in AI ultimately

create an artificial Superintelligence that is much smarter and faster than the collective
intelligence of humanity. As such, it can solve practical problems that remain unsolved by humans
and do it in a timely fashion. Superabundance is the logical implication of Superintelligence
as it works to create a world where economic goods, services and energy are essentially free
because of the omniscience implied by the most efficient processing of unlimited information by

superintelligent models. In essence, Superintelligence could mimic the Creation.
Some aspects of Superabundance include automated production of desired output that
eliminates most physical labor, clean energy and synthetic materials that eliminate resource
scarcity, and increased longevity as Superintelligence is applied to maintaining physical health
via, for example, synthetic organ replacement and personalized illness treatments. Obviously,
Superabundance pulls the rug out from under traditional economics, which is the study of how

scarce resources are allocated. While the "scarcity" vanishes in this new world, the allocation issue
remains and is likely to become the focus of political debate. In particular, the question of how
jobs and incomes fit into a superabundant world is likely to become a bigger and bigger issue.
The trend of technology replacing more and more of the labor share of income is already decades
old and dates back to the beginnings of digital technology. That trend has picked up speed

with the increasing profit share of output the new technologies created. The ratio of income
to the number of workers is orders of magnitude higher for the big new technology companies
compared to the old economy companies of the past. This means the owners of capital
(shareholders) are receiving a bigger share of the economic pie as high-margin, high-productivity
companies based in the new technology become a bigger part of the economy. This trend is

accelerating. That is a direct implication of the movement toward and through the Singularity.
It means the issue of income inequality, which has always been on society's radar screen, will
become bigger in the years ahead unless there are wise policies to share the superabundance
of the near future. Some countries are better prepared for this future than others. Norway,
for example, has built a sovereign wealth fund (SWF) over the past 25 years that represents a

substantial share of capital income for each of its citizens. In the U.S., with its widespread equity
culture, thanks to Individual Retirement Accounts and 401K plans, capital income is more widely
distributed compared to Europe with its much more debt-based capital markets. In any event,
widespread equity ownership is one way to deal with a superabundant economy where AI has
significantly reduced the labor share of income.
Demographics add to the natural evolution from labor income to capital income as societies

become wealthier. Aging societies of retirees by definition depend on capital income more than
labor income. This transition of the economy to higher margins and faster earnings growth is
the fortunate accompaniment to the need for more income from capital as societies become
older. The longevity trend naturally accelerates this process, which is the logical result of
technological progress raising productivity and living standards. People live longer in wealthy

societies, as subsistence constraints on life spans diminish while lifestyle and mental health
constraints become more the issue. How lifestyles and mental health evolve in a world where
work becomes less necessary is already showing signs of their increased importance for the
future of public health.
The current accelerating profits cycle reflects this impact of new technology on the relative
returns to labor and capital. The trend so far has been relatively slow compared to what's

coming if Superintelligence and Superabundance are the outcomes the Singularity promises as
increasingly seems likely. Because much financial analysis relies on past cycles the accelerating
Investment Implications
Rapid innovation, political upheaval
and increasing creative destruction
raise the difficulty of valuing
longer duration assets as we saw
earlier this year in the software
space. Higher-risk premia are a
likely result. On balance, this favors

Value-oriented assets over Growth.
Higher real yields in a strong capital
spending environment reinforce this
preference.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
profits and rising margins of the current economy have naturally focused attention on what
happens when conditions revert to the mean of lower margins and more historically normal
profit growth. Looked at through this lens, Equities appear overvalued and destined to correct
bubble-like conditions. The Singularity, however, implies "this time is different," the four most
dangerous words in investing. Yet, the slowly evolving shift toward capital income already under

way is an early warning sign of bigger things to come as these trends are turbo-charged to warp
speed while the economy passes through the Singularity. Seen this way, a super-cycle in profits,
margins and stock values seems logical.
A revamping of the social safety net to address the conditions implied by Superabundance
is already a hot topic in Silicon Valley. Various proposals for a universal basic income (UBI)
are being bandied about. Typically, UBI refers to the government giving everyone an income

sufficient to meet some living standard. This automatically implies a big role for government in
the new economy. Given the fourth-turning breakdown in the political process and governance,
alternatives that avoid the government sector might also be considered. For example, another
implication of the Singularity is an accelerating accumulation of wealth fueling a growing supply
of altruistic and philanthropic potential that arguably could be administered more efficiently by

the private sector than the government where widespread allegations of corrupt administration
at all levels have proliferated since the pandemic. Given the growing wealth pie and the diversity
of social priorities among its owners addressing more social needs through philanthropy is a
likely option for the superabundant economy. In any event, the relative roles of government and
the private sector are necessarily an issue to be hashed out in this revolutionary time.

More glimmers of the UBI-like movements have popped up recently. As mentioned, Norway's
SWF is an example of how a well-administered government has been preparing for this future.
In the U.S. government sponsored tax-advantaged savings accounts from birth have been
instituted with private donations being added to the program. Governments, including the U.S.,
have increasingly been buying stakes in private investments for various purposes with the result

that the focus solely on sovereign debt can be a misleading indicator of fiscal fragility.
Japan is a significant example of the difference between gross government debt and net
government debt after subtracting government owned assets. Its gross debt is around 279% of
its gross domestic product (GDP) which is much higher than any other major economy. However,
Japan government assets total around 192% of GDP , including domestic and foreign Equities,

bonds, and loans. As a result, its net debt is less than 100% of GDP and more in line with other
major economies. As one of the most aged societies in the world, Japan points the way toward
how societies can cope with longevity. Government ownership of a broad array of higher yielding
global assets funded with low sovereign debt interest rates appears to be a path the U.S.
Treasury is also starting to pursue in managing its debt burden.
The main risks to the uptopian promise of Superabundance are the dystopian possibilities

that would follow from poor social decision-making. Around the world, different governments
will follow different approaches to navigating the unprecedented challenges posed by the
Singularity. As the U.S. celebrates its 250-year anniversary, it is in the midst of its fourth "fourth
turning." These are the intense political upheaval periods that demographers and historians have
documented in every fourth generation. The Revolutionary War, the Civil War, and the Great

Depression and World War II mark the first three "fourth turnings." The Singularity is a major
marker for the current fourth turning.
The relative success of the U.S. in transitioning through the previous major turning points in
history provides hope for a utopian rather than a dystopian outcome. Other countries have not
transited these challenges so well for such an extended period which helps explain how the
U.S. has become the most prosperous country on earth. Nevertheless, past performance is no

guarantee of future success. As always, each generation faces new challenges to overcome, or not.
For investors, the Singularity seems to be behind an unusually strong earnings cycle that could
potentially last much longer than past history would suggest. That's positive for Equities and
risk assets in general. The extra risks associated with this rapid transformation argue for well
diversified holdings as creative destruction on steroids seems a plausible outcome in a world of

warp speed change.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Elevated Debt Supply Is Testing the Market's Appetite
Chief Investment Office, Fixed Income Strategy Team
For much of the past two decades, rising debt levels appeared to carry few
consequences. Federal deficits expanded and corporate borrowing increased, yet
Treasury yields generally trended lower and credit spreads stayed remarkably tame.
Today, the backdrop may be changing.

Federal borrowing needs continue to grow as deficit spending remains elevated. Net
interest expense has surpassed $1 trillion annually and now rivals total U.S. defense
spending. On this front there appears no end in sight as the Congressional Budget
Office projects that the fiscal 2026 shortfall will widen to roughly $2.1 trillion. Investors
are increasingly focused on not only the level of debt outstanding, but also the pace at
which new debt is being issued—with some questioning about the sustainability of this

situation over the longer term.
This phenomenon is not limited to Treasurys, as the issuance glut extends to corporate
debt. Investment-grade (IG) corporate issuance is running roughly 40% above last year
and is on pace for a record year. 1 While demand for Fixed Income remains healthy, the
sheer volume of new debt being brought to market is beginning to test investor capacity,
particularly at the long end of the Treasury curve and within certain sectors.

Exhibit 1: Low Yields Post-2008/2009 GFC* Have Fueled a Borrowing Binge.

Hyperscalers Are Competing for Long-Duration Capital. A significant driver of
the surge in corporate issuance has been the hyperscalers. The largest technology
companies are financing an unprecedented wave of AI-related capital spending, leading
to nearly $280 billion of debt issuance year-to-date. 2 Many of these issuers carry high
credit ratings and have ramped up issuance of longer-maturity bonds. As a result, they
are increasingly competing with long-dated Treasurys for the same pool of institutional

capital, including insurance companies, pension funds, sovereign wealth funds and
duration-oriented Fixed Income investors—creating a crowding-out effect amongst high-
quality Fixed Income.
Fortunately, this is a case of pressure, not a credit crisis. Recent weakness in long-dated
Treasurys reflects a classic supply-and-demand imbalance and importantly is not a
solvency crisis for the U.S. government or high-quality hyperscalers. Investors are being

asked to absorb a growing volume of net new issuance from both the U.S. Treasury and
corporate America at the same time.
Debt Pressure and a Normalization, Not a Fiscal Reckoning. The rise in long-end
yields during August reflects this dynamic. The 30-year Treasury yield moved above
5.3%, reaching levels not seen since 2007. Fiscal concerns likely played a role, but we

2 Source: Bloomberg as of September 9, 2026.

reflects a collision of fiscal co ncerns,
resilient growth, persistent inflation,
and record debt issuance from both

of market pressures, we view the
recent move as a normalization
process, not a cri sis of confidence .

### Page 5

5 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
believe heavy supply, sticky inflation, reduced Fed demand and increased competition for
investor capital were equally important contributors.
And importantly, the market's adjustment mechanism is already working. Higher Treasury
yields and somewhat wider credit spreads are attracting buyers and helping clear the growing
supply of bonds. This is a natural normalization and is how markets restore equilibrium.

Recent Treasury auctions continue to attract demand consistent with historical norms, and
investor appetite for new IG issuance has remained robust. These are not characteristics
typically associated with a loss of confidence in U.S., or corporate, creditworthiness.
A New Yield Regime. For Fixed Income investors, the more important implication
is that a 4% to 5% 10-year Treasury yield may represent the new normal. In an
environment of very strong underlying economic growth and unprecedented volumes of

debt issuance from both the public and private sectors, higher yields may be required to
attract sufficient capital amid a more competitive landscape for yield.
We continue to view both nominal and real yields as attractive and believe investors
should remain focused on achieving strategic duration targets. While rate volatility may
persist, today's yield levels provide a potential opportunity to seek attractive income that
has been largely absent for much of the past decade.

The Bigger Picture: Growth Still Matters More Than Debt Levels. None of this dismisses
the long-term fiscal challenges facing the U.S. Debt levels are rising, interest costs
continue to grow, and mandatory spending increasingly consumes a larger share of the
federal budget. However, history shows that debt levels alone do not determine interest
rates. Between 2007 and 2020, U.S. debt rose dramatically even as the 10-year Treasury
yield fell from roughly 5% to below 1%. What ultimately matters is the interplay between

short-term interest rates, debt levels, economic growth, inflation and investor demand.
For instance, nominal GDP growth and the 10-year yield tend to be correlated, outside
of recessions—we believe recent rate increases partly reflect this strong macro
backdrop. Moreover, economic growth is running above long-end yields, supporting the
sustainability of rising government debt.

The U.S. continues to benefit from several structural advantages: the dollar remains
the world's reserve currency, Treasurys remain the foundation of the global financial

system, and U.S. capital markets are still the deepest and most liquid in the world. Those
advantages continue to support demand for U.S. assets and underpin sovereign credit
quality even as borrowing needs grow.
The bottom line, in our view, is that even though the recent rise in long-end yields catches
many headlines, there is no need to panic. Instead, the recent repricing reflects periodic
episodes of debt pressure rather than a fundamental loss of confidence in U.S. credit. With

borrowing demand from both Washington and corporate America likely to remain elevated,
bond prices may continue to serve as the market's release valve. The consequence is likely
a world of structurally higher long-term yields, not a sovereign debt crisis.

### Page 6

6 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What are the markets actually telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investmen t Officer
When the markets speak loudly we should listen. There are often clues in the internal trends
within the markets that help us determine how far into an economic cycle we are or what drivers
of growth are most impactful. In addition, we can also find clues that help with determining

the micro picture. In other words, what industries are the most likely to benefit, from a
profitability standpoint is concerned, as the growth picture unfolds? Currently, the markets have
a megaphone, in our view. The year-to-date sharp outperformers have been consistent and
should continue to advance. These include commodities, including copper and oil, Emerging
Markets, Japan in the Developed Markets, Small-cap relative to Large-cap shares, Value versus

Growth, and more cyclically-oriented companies versus more defensive. This current consistent
outperformance informs us that we are still early- to mid-cycle in a supply-led expansion. We are
still early in the build out of the supply that is needed for the natural demand that is there. New
infrastructure is needed to handle the new economy that is progressing and quickly innovating.
This infrastructure needs commodities, all forms of energy, battery storage, minerals, metals,

recycled water, gases, chemicals, and all the compute and cloud capacity, including memory
and other components to handle the path toward mass tokenization. Moreover, the buildup of
government debt in the advanced economies as fiscal deficits widen is forcing large institutional
investors (including central banks and other government entities) to diversify their reserve
bases. This has increased the interest in gold holdings. Countries that have a vast amount of

natural resources, especially oil and gas, are in high demand by investors, which is obvious when
examining year-to-date outperformers. The bottom line is that the markets are telling us that we
are still early- to mid-cycle in this massive buildout, which will require a wide collection of hard
assets, Infrastructure (old and new), land, and within Equities, a more diversified approach to
exposure—particularly areas that are more cyclical- and Value-oriented. We expect this view to

extend itself well into the foreseeable future if not years.
In Fixed Income, it may seem more complex, but it is actually easier to understand what the
markets are telling us, in our opinion. The backup in
yields is “real yield” driven, which is more due
to higher nominal growth than to concerns of long-run inflation. This is evident in the Treasury
inflation protection market, break-evens in general and the ratio of Treasury yields relative to
other Go

vernment bond markets in the developed world. However, rising oil and gas prices and
inflation gauges that are still above the preferred level are usually the excuses for yields backing
up. Deficit concerns are a more plausible reason f
or yields rising but the main driver, in our view,
is still abo
ve average nominal growth. The yield curve and rates in general are still adjusting to
this new economic landscape that is very different than those of the most recent past.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Bloomberg Commodity Index designed to provide liquid and diversified exposure to commodities via futures contracts. Is calculated on an excess return basis and reflects commodity
futures price movements.
MSCI Emerging Markets Index captures large and mid cap representation across 24 Emerging Markets countries. With 1,205 constituents, the index covers approximately 85% of the free
float adjusted market capitalization in each country.
MSCI Japan Index is designed to measure the performance of the large and mid cap segments of the Japanese market.

MSCI Pacific ex Japan Index captures large and mid cap representation across 4 of 5 Developed Markets (DM) countries in the Pacific region (excluding Japan). With 93 constituents, the index
covers approximately 85% of the free float-adjusted market capitalization in each country.
MSCI All-Country World Index is a premier benchmark representing global equity performance, covering large and mid-cap stocks across 23 developed and 24 emerging markets.

MSCI World Index ex U.S. captures large and mid cap representation across 22 of 23 Developed Markets countries--excluding the United States..
MSCI USA Momentum Index is based on MSCI USA Index, its parent index, which captures large and mid-cap stocks of the U.S. market. It is designed to reflect the performance of an equity
momentum strategy by emphasizing stocks with high price momentum, while maintaining reasonably high trading liquidity, investment capacity and moderate index turnover.

U.S. Large-cap Growth/Russell 1000 Growth Index measures the performance of the large-cap growth segment of the U.S. equity universe. It includes those Russell 1000 companies with
relatively higher price-to-book ratios, higher I/B/E/S forecast medium term (2 year) growth and higher sales per share historical growth (5 years).
U.S. Small-cap/Russell 2000 Index measures the performance of the small-cap segment of the U.S. Equity universe.
MSCI USA High Dividend Yield Index is designed to reflect the performance of equities in the MSCI USA Index (excluding REIT s) with higher dividend income and quality characteristics than

average dividend yields that are both sustainable and persistent.
U.S. Mid Cap/Russell Midcap Index measures the performance of the mid-cap segment of the U.S. Equity universe. The Russell Midcap Index is a subset of the Russell 1000® Index. It
includes approximately 800 of the smallest securities based on a combination of their market cap and current index membership.
MSCI USA Value Index captures large and mid-cap U.S. securities exhibiting overall value style characteristics.

MSCI USA Equal Weighted Index represents an alternative weighting scheme to its market cap weighted parent index, the MSCI USA Index. At each quarterly rebalance date, all index
constituents are weighted equally, effectively removing the influence of each constituent’s current price.
MSCI USA Minimum Volatility Index aims to reflect the performance characteristics of a minimum variance strategy applied to the large and mid-cap USA equity universe.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

### Page 1

By Advanced Economies Team
Advanced Economies:
Resilience to shock after shock
October 2026

### Page 2

• The global economy is in the midst of a growth cycle. As a result, GDP growth is more resilient to shocks than initially
anticipated.
• The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise in oil prices, whose negative impact on growth is offset
by a series of reflationary forces.
• The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial intelligence (AI).
• The world’s major economies are currently engaged in a broader push to expand their capital goods investment.

• While European and US households are experiencing more or less the same energy price shock, inflation and household
consumption remain higher in the US.
• Higher and above-target inflation calls for monetary tightening.
• Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks examined in this publication (the US, the UK, the
Eurozone). Inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026 or Q1 2027, but there is a significant risk that energy inflation will

spread further to other components of inflation in the future.
• Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength (in the US).
• Monetary policies also have to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal policy (particularly in Japan).
• The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: the ensuing rise in energy prices contributes to the recent increase in
long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the start of the year) to levels close to those of 2002.

• The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period of growth), reflecting different fiscal trajectories. In the
US, the UK, France and Germany the public debt-to-GDP ratio is increasing, while it has stabilized in Italy and decreased in
Spain and Japan.
• The rise in long-term interest rates will weigh on public finances. The fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it will be in 2030.
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with GDP growth.

For more information, please refer to our publication:
EcoPerspectives – Advanced Economies | 3rd quarter of 2026, as of September 24, 2026– Economic Research– BNP Paribas

## Document 296: GF - PTFE Adoption

Source PDF: all report pdf/GF - PTFE Adoption.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-18
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Equity Research 
Sector Report | Overseas Technology｜Oct 15,2026 
PTFE Adoption: 
Reshape the High-End Materials Landscape

Overseas  
Technology

Michelle Jing 
SFC CE No. BUK594 
michellejing@gfgroup.com.hk 
Jeff Pu, CFA 
SFC CE No. BNO719 
jeffpu@gfgroup.com.hk

What's new 
Nvidia showcased its NVLink PTFE board prototype for the first time at OCP Global Summit 2026, highlighting seven supply-chain partners: 
Taiflex (8039 TT), Flexium (6269 TT), TTM Technologies (TTMI US), 3 leading Chinese PCB manufacturers, and 1 leading Chinese CCL 
supplier. We believe this development reinforces our previous view that PTFE remains the most promising material solution for the VR300 
NVL576 switch board, offering the best balance between electrical performance and MP feasibility. 
Comments 
PTFE hybrid remains the preferred solution: As highlighted in our Oct. 5 report, we continue to favor the PTFE hybrid solution for the 
VR300 NVL576 switch board among the various options, given its optimal balance between electrical performance and MP feasibility. 
While pure PTFE remains challenging to process, particularly in lamination and drilling, leading PCB manufacturers have achie ved 
encouraging yields with hybrid structures that combine PTFE signal layers with M9 materials in non-signal layers. 
We like PTFE's long-term adoption potential: PTFE applications in high-frequency and high-speed interconnects could extend beyond 
switch boards. The IEC has published a standard on PTFE-filled CCL, led by the leading Chinese CCL company, highlighting the material's 
relevance to broader circuit-board applications. Our checks also suggest that PTFE materials have entered the testing phase for other 
applications, including compute boards. We therefore remain positive on PTFE's long-term adoption across a broader range of high-end 
PCB applications. 
Limited near-term impact on conventional resin materials, but long-term substitution risks warrant attention: Under the current 
PTFE hybrid design, we expect 8–12 out of 32 layers to adopt PTFE for signal transmission, accounting for less than 50% of the total CCL 
resin content value. Over the longer term, we expect a potential shift toward pure PTFE solutions as improvements in processing 
technology enable commercially viable MP yields, reducing the need for hybrid structures. This could gradually increase substitution risks 
for conventional high-end PCB resin materials, including PPO and HC resin. 
Favor PTFE-exposed suppliers with proven technical and manufacturing capabilities: We remain positive on PTFE-related 
opportunities, particularly suppliers with strong R&D capabilities, proven MP expertise, and established customer qualifications. In CCL, 
we favor Taiflex (8039 TT) and leading Chinese CCL supplier for their PTFE material capabilities and supply-chain exposure. Among the 
five Nvidia-announced PCB suppliers, we favor the leading Chinese PTFE PCB supplier, given its advanced hybrid PTFE processing 
capabilities and encouraging yields. For CCL suppliers that have yet to enter the PTFE supply chain, we recommend monitoring progress 
in developing, while remaining alert to potential market-share losses in their conventional product portfolios. 
Risks  
1) AI demand slowing down; 2) Geo-political risks; 3) Competition.

## Document 373: Top of Mind_ Higher bond yields_ how concerning_

Source PDF: all report pdf/Top of Mind_ Higher bond yields_ how concerning_.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-10-13
Pages: 31
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
Global bond yields have risen to multi-decade highs, yet growth and markets have
remained remarkably resilient. But with some pressures beginning to emerge (e.g. in
European sovereign bond markets), can this resilience last, or will more vulnerabilities
be revealed? We ask the IMF’s Tobias Adrian, Moody’s Analytics’ Mark Zandi, and GS
economists. Adrian is relatively sanguine about the economy’s resilience, though
notes downside risks, while GS economists expect only a modest growth drag from

higher rates, but a more consequential one if yields remain near current or higher
levels. Zandi is generally more concerned, describing the US economy as vulnerable
rather than resilient, and sees one-in-three odds of the economy going off the rails.
Meanwhile, GS strategists and analysts are not especially concerned about banks or
markets more broadly, but warn that further increases in yields or rate volatility could
test risk assets. Key to watch: whether the increasingly interconnected and leveraged

AI investment boom can continue to withstand higher rates.
Tobias Adrian, Special Advisor and former Director of Monetary and Capital Markets, IMF
“The global economy has demonstrated considerable resilience in the face of multiple severe shocks, and I would not be
surprised if it continues to shrug off higher yields, provided they do not keep rising sharply in a disorderly fashion. At the same
time, I do worry about downside risks.”
Mark Zandi, Chief Economist, Moody’s Analytics

We provide a brief snapshot on the most important economies for the global markets

### Page 3

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 3 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Recent acceleration in the rise in global bond yields to multi-
decade highs has begun to raise questions about the 
implications for growth and markets. Both have remained 
remarkably resilient so far, with US growth in particular 
holding relatively firm and the S&P 500 and other equity 
markets near new all-time highs. But with pressure 
beginning to emerge—most notably in European sovereign 
bond markets—and yields looking unlikely to retreat soon, 
can that resilience last, or will more vulnerabilities be revealed? 
GS Head of US Rates Strategy William Marshall first lays out 
how we arrived at higher rates, underscoring the striking 
breadth of the rise, which he attributes mainly to 
fundamentals—strong growth, inflation risks, hawkish policy 
shifts, the ongoing investment cycle, and long-run fiscal 
risks—but notes that technical factors likely amplified the 
recent move. And while he ultimately expects yields to fall 
(with YE 10-year US Treasury yields of 4.75%/4.4% in 
2026/2027, respectively) as markets become more confident 
in easing inflation, he thinks elevated long-term yields should 
have some staying power, and sees continued upside risk 
from energy price and fiscal pressures.  
So, just how much of a threat do higher rates pose to the 
macro outlook? For answers, we first turn to Tobias Adrian, 
Special Advisor to the Managing Director and former 
Financial Counsellor and Director of the Monetary and Capital 
Markets Department at the IMF, Mark Zandi, Chief 
Economist of Moody’s Analytics, and GS US economists. 
Adrian sees a global economy that has so far responded to 
the yield shock much as it has to the many other major 
shocks of the past seven years: resiliently. He notes that the 
rise in yields has not significantly tightened financial 
conditions or, more importantly, disrupted market functioning 
or forced damaging deleveraging. He believes that resilience 
could persist as long as the move in yields doesn’t accelerate 
or become more disorderly. But he is concerned about 
vulnerabilities in the AI and technology complex, sovereign 
bond markets, and EMs—not to mention cyber risks—all of 
which leaves him “vigilant but cautiously optimistic.” 
GS US economists also characterize the US economy as 
resilient. Their expectation that the Fed will deliver just one 
more rate hike this year before cutting next year—consistent 
with Marshall’s expectation of lower Treasury yields—leads 
Pierfrancesco Mei to expect only a modest 0.2pp growth 
drag in 2027 from higher rates. But he finds that the drag 
could rise to a more consequential 0.5pp if yields remain near 
current levels (see pg. 9 for GS global economists Joseph 
Briggs’ and Johan Allen’s rules of thumb for the impact of 
higher yields on rate-sensitive areas across major DMs.) 
David Mericle and Mei also worry about the implications for 
the already-concerning US fiscal trajectory, which they 
believe is unlikely to trigger a classic debt crisis but is too risky 
to ignore.     
Zandi, for his part, is generally more concerned. While his 
baseline outlook is relatively sanguine, he describes the US 
economy as vulnerable rather than resilient and views the 
“distribution of possible outcomes as very flat and heavily 
skewed toward darker tail scenarios.” He sees the clearest 
softness in the labor market, with weak hiring and falling real 
wage growth for many workers weighing on consumer 
spending. And he takes little comfort from US growth 
continuing to run around 2%—roughly its potential rate—
noting that AI accounts for roughly one-third of that growth, 
leaving the non-AI economy growing at only around 1-1.5%, 
which he calls “a pretty punk economy.” Zandi also worries 
that the US fiscal situation is “as bad as I have ever seen it” 
and that higher rates are already inflicting damage on real 
estate markets and squeezing existing debtors. As these 
stresses mount, he sees elevated risk of something breaking 
in the financial system. All told, he puts the odds of the 
current vulnerabilities pushing the economy completely off 
the rails at around one-in-three.  
With banks historically at the center of the rate shocks and 
economic crises that Zandi is worried about, we then explore 
how much risk banks pose today. Adrian and GS Head of the 
Financials Equity Group Richard Ramsden both see little 
cause for alarm, explaining that higher rates should boost 
banks’ net interest income and revenues given their asset-
sensitive balance sheets. Ramsden also doesn’t expect a 
significant deterioration in credit quality absent a broader 
economic downturn and says that most banks should have 
excess capital even after accounting for rate-driven portfolio 
losses.   
But even if banks can weather higher rates, what about 
markets more broadly? GS strategists and analysts are not 
especially concerned, with Chief US Equity Strategist Ben 
Snider arguing that current rate levels shouldn’t prevent 
continued strong US earnings growth and the S&P 500 from 
climbing higher, Chief Credit Strategist Amanda Lynam 
viewing the headwind to corporate credit from higher rates 
as manageable amid the solid growth backdrop, and US 
Financials Analyst Alexander Blostein expecting the recent 
slowdown in private market activity to rebound once rates 
stabilize. GS multi-asset strategists Christian Mueller-
Glissmann and Andrea Ferrario add that higher yields have 
strengthened the strategic case for bonds in a multi-asset 
portfolio, though strong earnings continue to favor equities 
for now. But they all warn that if rate volatility remains 
elevated, and especially if yields continue to rise from here, 
risk assets could face mounting pressures.  
All that said, with the AI boom powering the economy and 
markets, everything may come down to whether higher rates 
will derail it. Snider views this as unlikely given the strong 
balance sheets of the firms investing in AI and the expected 
profitability of these investments, with Lynam also seeing a 
high bar to derail AI-related debt issuance. Adrian also 
maintains a positive baseline for the AI economy but is 
concerned about the circularity of investment, increased 
leverage, and the sustainability of revenue growth in the AI 
complex. And Zandi again seems the most worried, warning 
that “AI could become the part of the economy that breaks” 
if rates remain at current levels for much longer.  
Allison Nathan, Editor  
Email: allison.nathan@gs.com     
Tel: 212-357-7504   
Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC

Higher bond yields: how concerning?

### Page 4

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 4 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Mark Zandi is Chief Economist of Moody’s Analytics. Below, he argues that the US economy 
is vulnerable rather than resilient and worries that high interest rates could deepen existing 
pockets of weakness and ultimately cause something to break in the financial system.   
The views stated herein are those of the interviewee and do not necessarily reflect those of Goldman Sachs.
Allison Nathan: Prior to the 
September 2026 Fed rate hike, you 
argued that a hike would do little to 
address supply-driven inflation but 
could damage already weak parts of 
the US economy. Where are you 
seeing the most softness? 
Mark Zandi: The clearest softness is 
in the labor market. Monthly payroll 
growth has averaged around 40,000 over the past year and has 
been essentially zero when excluding the healthcare sector. 
Hiring is moribund, hours worked are very low, and while the 
unemployment rate remains low, that is partly a reflection of 
declining labor force participation. Adjusting for that cyclical 
drop would put underlying unemployment in the mid-4% 
range—above the level consistent with full employment—and I 
expect the headline rate to move toward that level over the 
next 6-18 months. Real wage growth is, at best, flat, and for 
many Americans is actually declining, which is eroding 
purchasing power, particularly for lower- and middle-income 
households, who are increasingly drawing down savings to 
sustain spending. So, while the economy is still growing, the 
underlying labor market and household fundamentals point to 
very tenuous growth. 
 While the economy is still growing, the 
underlying labor market and household 
fundamentals point to very tenuous growth.”   
Allison Nathan: What is causing this softness? 
Mark Zandi: Policy choices are at the root of the weakness. 
Tariffs, while more consequential last year, continue to weigh 
on growth and boost inflation. Heavy-handed immigration policy 
is also an underappreciated source of weakness. Many firms 
losing immigrant workers cannot afford to raise wages enough 
to attract native-born workers, so they are raising prices to align 
demand with the supply they can produce with a reduced labor 
force. But the most immediate and significant driver of the 
softness is the Iran war, which has pushed up energy prices, 
further fueling inflation and weighing on growth.  
Allison Nathan: But US real GDP growth remains near 2%—
roughly the economy’s potential rate. Isn’t that a relatively 
healthy growth pace? 
Mark Zandi: Real GDP growth is indeed running at 2% yoy, but 
I estimate that AI-related activity accounts for roughly one-third 
of that, or around 0.6–0.7pp. Roughly half of that comes from AI 
capital spending, net of electronics imports for data centers, 
while the balance reflects wealth effects from the surge in AI-
related stock prices that have contributed to an almost doubling 
in the S&P 500 since ChatGPT debuted four years ago, boosting 
net worth and consumer spending, especially among high-
income households. So, the non-AI economy is growing at only 
around 1%–1.5%. That’s a pretty punk economy, and a difficult 
place to be. 
Allison Nathan: The September SEP indicated that most 
FOMC members expect one more rate hike this year, and 
the market is expecting the same. How damaging could 
further tightening be? 
Mark Zandi: While I expect another hike this year, with the 
economy already barely growing at potential, additional hikes 
could push growth below potential. That would further strain 
the already weak non-AI economy and trigger layoffs, 
significantly raising the likelihood of a self-reinforcing negative 
spiral. So, further tightening would be a risky endeavor. 
 With the economy already barely 
growing at potential, additional hikes could 
push growth below potential. That would 
further strain the already weak non-AI 
economy and trigger layoffs, significantly 
raising the likelihood of a self-reinforcing 
negative spiral.”   
Higher rates are already inflicting damage on the economy’s 
most rate-sensitive sectors, particularly housing and 
commercial real estate. Refinancing is in a deep freeze. Existing 
home sales have fallen to levels last seen during the teeth of 
the housing crash and the Global Financial Crisis. Home prices 
are moving sideways on average and outright declining in half 
the country. That could easily create broader problems for 
households, especially for those that bought homes in the last 
years after rates began to rise and have virtually no home 
equity. And while commercial real estate had begun to show 
signs of life, renewed rate increases are once again freezing 
activity and putting pressure on property values.  
Higher rates are also squeezing existing debtors as borrowing 
costs on credit cards, home loans, auto loans, and corporate 
debt rise. I am particularly worried about highly leveraged, often 
private equity-backed companies that have managed the higher 
rate environment by extending maturities and limiting near-term 
interest payments. That strategy cannot continue indefinitely if 
borrowing costs remain elevated or continue to rise.  
The equity market is another key area of concern. AI continues 
to propel the equity market, but it is not impervious to higher 
rates. Persistently elevated rates will eventually weigh on 
valuations, increasing the risk of a major correction that could 
quickly reverberate through the real economy as positive wealth 
effects reverse and consumer spending weakens. AI capex is 
also vulnerable as a higher cost of capital makes investment 
Interview with Mark Zandi

### Page 5

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 5 
Top of Mind Issue 152

more expensive and falling valuations make it harder to raise 
the capital necessary to sustain the current pace of the AI 
buildout. For now, AI is operating under its own momentum, 
supported by considerable optimism and euphoria. But if rates 
remain at these levels or higher for longer than a quarter or two, 
AI could become the part of the economy that breaks. 
Last but not least, higher rates will worsen the US fiscal 
situation, which is itself contributing to elevated yields today. 
While we have navigated this fiscal risk for some time, the 
fiscal situation is as bad as I have ever seen it. The deficit 
exceeds 6% of GDP, the primary deficit is above 3%, and debt 
is roughly 100% of GDP and on track to reach 125% in a 
decade. Annual federal interest payments are already well 
above $1tn and will rise rapidly if rates remain elevated. All told, 
I see a roughly one-in-five chance of a fiscal cliff event over the 
coming year—unlikely, but large enough to warrant attention. 
So, higher rates will weigh on the economy through several 
channels. Historically, rate increases of this magnitude have 
almost invariably caused something to break in the financial 
system. We cannot know exactly where the fault line will 
emerge, but the risk of these pockets of weakness becoming a 
broader problem is significant. 
 I view the distribution of possible 
outcomes as very flat and heavily skewed 
toward darker tail scenarios.”  
Allison Nathan: How do these risks translate into your 
forecasts for economic growth? 
Mark Zandi: My baseline forecast is relatively sanguine based 
on the assumption that the Iran war begins to wind down, 
allowing oil prices to decline to around $80/bbl as we move into 
next year. That should ease inflation, reduce Fed tightening 
concerns, and bring the 10-year Treasury yield back toward 
4.5% by early next year—a level the economy can digest.  
Importantly, though, I view the distribution of possible 
outcomes as very flat and heavily skewed toward darker tail 
scenarios. The war may not fade into the background, keeping 
energy prices elevated and raising the risk of further inflation 
pressure. That could prompt additional Fed tightening, push 
yields even higher, and ultimately cause something to break in 
the financial system.  
Meanwhile, the Treasury market has grown more fragile as 
traditional, price-insensitive buyers—including the Fed, banks, 
and foreign institutional investors—have stepped back, 
increasing the market’s reliance on highly leveraged, price-
sensitive hedge funds that could rush for the exits under stress. 
A debt-limit confrontation under a likely divided US government 
next year could provide the catalyst, particularly if lawmakers 
underestimate the consequences of a breach. In that scenario, 
an even sharper bond market selloff than we are currently 
experiencing could occur. While none of this is my baseline—
and I remain inherently optimistic because avoiding the darkest 
scenarios is largely a policy choice—I put the odds of a severe 
bond market selloff at around one-in-four and of the economy 
going completely off the rails for any of the reasons we have 
discussed at around one-in-three. So, the downside risks are 
significant. 
 I put the odds of a severe bond market 
selloff at around one-in-four and of the 
economy going completely off the rails for 
any of the reasons we have discussed at 
around one-in-three. So, the downside risks 
are significant.”  
Allison Nathan: The Fed has considerable room to cut 
rates. Should that provide any comfort that the worst-case 
scenarios may be avoided? 
Mark Zandi: That remains an open question. If inflation fades 
and allows the Fed to take its foot off the brake, rate cuts 
should bring yields down across the curve—though not back to 
pre-war levels. But in a darker scenario involving a debt limit 
crisis and even higher yields, short-term rate cuts would have 
little effect on the long end. The Fed would need to turn to 
alternative policy tools, including quantitative easing and bond 
purchases. That would amount to financial repression—a 
strategy similar to the one employed by the BoJ for many 
years—which could prove counterproductive by reducing the 
pressure on lawmakers to address the underlying problems 
while fueling even more persistent inflation. 
Allison Nathan: Why do you think others are more 
sanguine about the risks that higher interest rates pose to 
the US economy? 
Mark Zandi: Some likely see greater underlying economic 
strength and expect AI to drive much stronger growth. Others 
may believe that the neutral rate is higher, perhaps for the 
same reasons, while some may be more concerned about 
persistently higher inflation.  
But the more fundamental difference is the broader lens 
through which I currently view the economy. Many continue to 
describe the US economy as resilient, which I find fascinating, 
bordering on frustrating. In my view, the right word is 
vulnerable. Again, growth is pedestrian, the labor market is soft, 
which was further underscored by the much weaker-than- 
expected September jobs report, real wage growth is 
decelerating, inflation remains elevated, and interest rates are 
rising. That shift in perspective completely changes how one 
evaluates the risks ahead.

### Page 6

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 6 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Tobias Adrian is Special Advisor to the Managing Director and former Financial Counsellor and 
Director of the Monetary and Capital Markets Department at the IMF. Below, he argues that 
the global economy has been resilient despite higher bond yields and could very well remain 
so. But he sees several areas of concern in the economy and markets.  
The views stated herein are those of the interviewee and do not necessarily reflect those of Goldman Sachs, the IMF, IMF 
Management, or the IMF Executive Board.
Jenny Grimberg: The IMF has 
cautioned that sharp increases in 
global bond yields could create 
strains across economies and 
markets. How would you assess the 
global economy’s response to the 
recent rise in yields?  
Tobias Adrian: The global economy 
has so far responded to the rise in 
yields much the same way as it has to the shocks of the past 
seven years: resiliently. If somebody had told you in late 2019 
that the global economy would face a pandemic, multiple wars, 
deep geopolitical fragmentation, and trade and energy supply 
shocks, you would have been hard-pressed not to expect a 
global recession. The pandemic did, of course, trigger a 
contraction, but it was exceptionally short-lived despite the 
severe supply and demand shocks. And the global monetary 
tightening cycle that began in 2021 and accelerated sharply in 
2022 in response to the pandemic-related inflation surge did not 
trigger a recession at all, which is unusual by historical 
standards. So, the global economy has proven deeply resilient.   
Jenny Grimberg: What has that resilience owed to? 
Tobias Adrian: Three factors explain this resilience: policy, the 
adaptability of the economy, and some luck related to 
technological innovation. Over the past seven years, fiscal and 
monetary policymakers acted aggressively to stabilize economic 
activity. Fiscal policy provided substantial support during the 
pandemic, while central banks cut interest rates, expanded 
asset purchases, and established emergency standing facilities. 
That fiscal support has of course come at a significant long-term 
cost as debt-to-GDP ratios in advanced economies have surged 
since 2019, and deficits in many countries remain large, 
contributing to the rise in long-term yields. But policy has 
undoubtedly been critical to the economy’s resilience to date.  
The economy itself has also become more adaptable. A 
dividend of the pandemic is that firms have developed a greater 
ability to rewire global value chains quickly in response to a 
range of disruptions, from shifts in energy and food prices to 
geopolitical developments, which has helped the global 
economy more effectively absorb shocks.  
And luck has played a role in that the more recent challenges 
have coincided with the emergence and rapid adoption of 
generative AI technology, which has helped fuel expectations of 
a broad productivity boom and created massive demand for 
investment that is stimulating aggregate demand. If large 
language models (LLMs) had not come to the fore in November 
2022, global growth in the more recent period certainly would 
not have been as strong.  
Jenny Grimberg: As bond yields continue to climb, are you 
concerned that this resilience could falter? 
Tobias Adrian: So far, the rise in bond yields has not triggered 
a substantial, broad-based tightening in financial conditions or, 
more importantly, disruptions to market functioning or forced 
deleveraging that could damage markets and the economy. But 
I see three main areas of concern: the AI and technology 
complex, sovereign bond markets, and emerging markets (EMs).  
Three vulnerabilities stand out in the AI and technology 
complex. First, the interconnectivity and circularity of 
investment among many AI firms raises the risk that one firm’s 
earnings hit could fairly quickly spread across the broader 
complex. Second, many AI firms have taken on significant 
leverage, and investor leverage has also grown through the use 
of leveraged ETFs and derivatives. Leverage upon leverage can 
be problematic when adverse shocks hit. Third, while relatively 
subdued valuations suggest that the market is not in a 1999 
state of indiscriminate price appreciation, with investors 
continuing to differentiate across firms and sectors, the 
strength of the complex is predicated on strong earnings and 
high revenue growth, and it is not clear how long that will last. 
While my baseline outlook is positive, greater competition from 
open-source models, for example, could eat into AI firms’ 
profitability and pressure the broader value chain.    
Sovereign debt markets are another key concern. As I 
mentioned, global public debt is approaching 100% of GDP, and 
debt levels in major advanced economies exceed 120% of 
GDP. Those are staggering figures from a historical perspective. 
The investor base for sovereign bonds has also increasingly 
shifted to more leveraged investors who are more sensitive to 
macro developments. A key indicator to watch is auction tails—
the difference between the yield at which a government sells 
bonds at auction and the yield the market expected beforehand. 
Looking at 30-year bond auctions across G4 sovereign bond 
markets, the tails have grown over time, suggesting increased 
market vulnerability to supply. 5-to-10-year forward term premia 
have risen to pre-Global Financial Crisis (GFC) levels. So, the 
quantitative easing period has now been fully priced out of the 
long end of the global yield curve. And I see no good reason for 
term premia to decline in the foreseeable future. If anything, 
both expected short rates and the term premium will probably 
remain somewhat elevated.  
Finally, while EM economies have shown tremendous 
resilience through the shocks of the past seven years owing to 
stronger policy frameworks, resilient FX reserve buffers, and 
the development of local-currency bond markets that have 
reduced currency mismatches, vulnerabilities remain. 
Particularly striking is the K-shaped pattern of capital flows. 
Capital has flowed into EM sovereign bonds, while equity 
Interview with Tobias Adrian

### Page 7

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 7 
Top of Mind Issue 152

capital has flowed out on net as domestic investors direct their 
savings into global—and, in particular, US—technology stocks. 
So, even though EM equities have rallied over the past two 
years, capital flows have moved in the other direction. That 
leaves these countries’ external positions more leveraged, with 
less equity and more debt on net.  
Jenny Grimberg: Higher yields played a key role in the 2023 
regional banking crisis. Are you worried about banks today? 
Tobias Adrian: Not particularly. Banks generally do well when 
interest rates are elevated, because financial intermediation is 
fundamentally about spreads. When rates rise, asset yields 
often reprice faster than deposit costs, supporting bank net 
revenues. That partly explained the banking system’s overall 
resilience in 2023, even as a few outlier banks ran into trouble. 
Significant capital and liquidity buffers broadly absorbed mark-
to-market losses on fixed-income securities, while higher 
spreads supported profitability.  
Jenny Grimberg: What about residential and commercial 
real estate, where stresses were already visible? 
Tobias Adrian: Real estate markets are faring differently across 
countries. London’s housing market, for example, has cooled 
meaningfully, while other major financial centers like New York 
and Hong Kong continue to see strong home price appreciation. 
Real estate valuations are sensitive to long-term yields, and the 
pace of price appreciation will probably slow if yields remain at 
elevated levels. But with economic activity remaining relatively 
strong, immediate severe distress in either commercial or 
residential real estate seems unlikely.   
Jenny Grimberg: When would you begin to worry that 
higher yields are creating a financial stability problem?  
Tobias Adrian: The speed and magnitude of the yield move 
matter enormously. So far, even though bond yields have risen 
sharply, market functioning has remained orderly and no forced 
deleveraging appears to be occurring, as we discussed. I would 
be more concerned if the move in yields accelerated or became 
nonlinear, which could set off a damaging deleveraging spiral in 
which investors are forced to reduce leverage, adding to selling 
pressures and intensifying market stress. The question then 
becomes: who will catch the falling knife?  
And leveraged investors may not be the only source of selling 
pressures. Before central banks launched massive asset 
purchase programs to stabilize markets in March 2020, open-
ended funds were the sellers, while some hedge funds actually 
played a stabilizing role. Sharp selling pressure can even come 
from plain-vanilla mutual funds or ETFs. That said, it is 
reassuring that the major banks are well capitalized today, 
because it would be particularly worrisome if stress in the non-
bank sector coincided with forced asset sales in the banking 
sector. We saw that dynamic during the GFC and the Long-
Term Capital Management crisis.  
Jenny Grimberg: What should investors watch for warning 
signs of systemic stress? 
Tobias Adrian: I would watch repo market and money market 
functioning, because the early warning signs of stress typically 
show up in short-term funding markets. I would also look at a 
range of market-based indicators, including cross-currency and 
domestic basis measures, swap spreads, and convenience 
yields. I would also watch for a broader tightening of financial 
conditions, which can indicate that pressure is spreading 
beyond a particular market. And I would keep a close eye on 
implied volatility as a market-based assessment of risk that 
feeds directly into investors’ risk-management models. When 
implied volatility rises sharply, investors may need to cut 
positions, which can reinforce the underlying market move.   
Jenny Grimberg: If vulnerabilities were to become more 
acute, how well prepared are policymakers to respond? 
Tobias Adrian: Central banks retain a powerful ability to inject 
liquidity through repo operations, outright asset purchases, and 
emergency standing facilities, and in recent years they have 
repeatedly demonstrated that they can act quickly and 
decisively when market functioning deteriorates. That is distinct 
from the broader stance of monetary policy. Ongoing 
inflationary pressures mean that monetary policy remains on a 
tightening bias in many countries. But central banks can hike 
rates and inject liquidity at the same time.  
Fiscal policymakers are more constrained. Governments may 
still be willing to respond as forcefully as they did during the 
pandemic, but after taking such aggressive fiscal steps, less 
room exists to go big on fiscal policy today. Markets may also 
react to such support more negatively than in 2020 given 
already-elevated debt levels and ongoing concerns about fiscal 
sustainability. If monetary and fiscal authorities act together, as 
they did in 2020, that would temporarily mitigate market stress, 
but it would not resolve the longer-term fiscal problem.  
Hopefully, the recent rise in bond yields will feed back into the 
politics of fiscal policy and prompt an adjustment. Bond market 
pressure has already triggered policy shifts in some countries. 
The question is how far yields must rise before adjustment 
occurs more broadly, and whether that adjustment can happen 
without uncomfortable volatility. But until it occurs, fiscal 
policymakers will remain relatively constrained.     
Jenny Grimberg: Where does all of that leave you on the 
outlook for the global economy and markets? 
Tobias Adrian: It is important to distinguish between the 
baseline and the downside. My baseline is fairly positive. The 
global economy has demonstrated considerable resilience in 
the face of multiple severe shocks, and I would not be 
surprised if it continues to shrug off higher yields, provided they 
do not keep rising sharply in a disorderly fashion. At the same 
time, I do worry about downside risks. Beyond the 
vulnerabilities we have discussed, cyber risk is an important 
concern. So far, cyber risks have not been macro-critical, but 
they certainly could become so. AI could strengthen cyber 
defenses, but it could also enhance offensive capabilities. And 
while sophisticated institutions may be resilient to cyberattacks, 
many firms may lack the capabilities and know-how to defend 
themselves effectively. So, plenty of room exists to worry 
about the downside, but the offset is policymaking, especially 
monetary policymaking, as we have discussed. All of that 
leaves me vigilant but cautiously optimistic.
